> ​​@lolono8887 that's not true. There is a lot more archaeological evidence for Chinese battles, its just not widely reported precisely because so many Westerners are in denial that archaeological records for their own foundational myths are so sparse. Just to demonstrate the vast scale of difference - while we have evidence of an entire Chinese army existing and being recreated in clay form from the Qin dynasty (the Terracotta Army), we actually don't even have any evidence of what a complete legion actually looked like. There are multiple contradictory accounts, and there are strong indications there simply was never a standard legion to begin with despite all the talk that the legion was some kind of new unique permanent formation.

As an Italian, I was really looking forward to this class. You might expect our schools to teach a biased version of Roman history, but from the very beginning we’re taught that much of what we read needs to be interpreted through the same lens presented here (for example, the Aeneid, a foundational myth created to justify Rome’s origins and identity) I’m really enjoying this course. The bit where Muzio Scaevola burns his hand was hilarious and took me right back to my high school days. 

Thanks again, professor!

> Most of history is in fact very poorly sourced and fact-checked. For example, until recently every single history book mis-identifies who were the Marines in the famous Iwo Jima flag-raising photo. Indeed, the most famous of those Marines - the one who wrote Flags of Our Fathers which was turned into a movie - was NOT in the photo and it was actually someone else.  
>   
> What made it worse? It wasn't even professional historians who caught the mistake, since most were discouraged from "controversial" opinions. It was instead a couple of guys on the Internet who really liked Marine uniforms who provided the undeniable evidence that the official long-held narrative was wrong. And people attacked them for "dishonoring veterans" and the usual such nonsense until the Marines and the survivors themselves acknowledged the mistake and corrected it.  
>   
> A professional historian is frankly not a profession people should look up to for credibility. They instead simply repeat what those in power want the people to believe. Thats why a lot of the better historical content that has come out in the past decade has come from amateurs.

> @thomasellysonting3554  Honestly, this is kind of what I meant in my original comment. A lot of early Roman history is built on layers of myth, later retellings, and patchy evidence, so any intro course — whether in Italy or abroad — has to pick a narrative that’s simplified and digestible. It doesn’t mean historians are useless or lying, just that the sources themselves are limited and sometimes contradictory.  
>   
> That’s why things like Cannae, early Roman society, or the foundation stories always come with a mix of “here’s what we know” and “here’s what we think based on the evidence.” It’s not controversial, it’s just how ancient history works.  
>   
> So yeah, it’s totally fair to question the details — but that’s exactly the mindset Italian schools try to teach too: don’t take ancient writers at face value, and always keep the limits of the evidence in mind.  

> **@thomasellysonting3554**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y-gXVzwbFMU&lc=Ugwns2HMNImGVzycP8d4AaABAg.AQ-1eSyCvN9AQgnZ14NPQG)  
>
> ​​@fmrossi1 but it is controversial outside of small academic circles, because everyone wants there to be a boogeyman named Hannibal and an ultimate battle ala Cannae.  
>   
> In reality, the only piece of solid evidence we have of Hannibal's existence from a source written down during his time was the funeral eulogy for Fabius, which actually contradicts Polybius as the eulogy clearly states Fabius stormed Hannibal's main base in Italy and seized his treasury. This is an account more in line with Plutarch, but lots of people also dismiss Plutarch because he was pro-Caesar and pro-Fabius, when in reality Plutarch has a more coherent narrative of how successful generals in the ancient era operated (namely, they sat around and waited; refusing to fight until they had a clear advantage).

It would completly ignore Roman recods (the fasti Capitolini) where it show the death of both consuls as well as deaths of many other importnat senators and eques, in the same year as the battle of Cannae. The disasterous battle would also explain emergency elections next year and in the same period (recorded both by Livy and reflection from the fasti), the Romans appointing multiple dictators, proconsuls, and praetors to replace fallen commanders and raise new armies.  It also explains the context of war, where Italian cities are changing sides and joining Hannibal. The reason why we lack archeological records is because the are become farmland and remains of battle were wiped out, yet we still identified remains of roman camp and weapons to conclude that in the area was a large battle.

> In short, millions of people today believe Bradley was in the Iwo Jima photo, and the media depicting him as such is still in circulation like the books and movie. But it is all completely false. He is not there.  
>   
> With Hannibal I would agree he probably existed, but I would not in any way believe the Polybius narrative. It is literally self-contradictory, which if you examine closely is indicative of doublethink. Its written by someone already divorced from reality and cannot tell reality from falsehood. Instead its all just pushing an agenda - like how Ben Shapiro keeps claiming to be a patriotic American, yet at every turn would put another country ahead of America.  
>   
> Also regarding Hannibal in an Etruscan grave: There is only one example of this, and we aren't even sure its actually Hannibal. There is actually very little verified mentions of Hannibal - instead people assume it must be Hannibal whenever they find a Punic coin - and the one solid Roman inscription mentioning him actually says he was beaten multiple times in Italy which contradicts the Polybius/Scipio narrative.  
>   
> Again, it may indicate Hannibal exists, but its far from proof he was the scourge of Rome and yet also the new leader they installed to lead Carthage after winning the Punic War. In any case, being both simultaneously is nonsensical.

> @thomasellysonting3554 Hannibal was beaten in Italy in Polybius narrative as well. Did you read the book? He lost severely at Metapontium, Lucania, Samnium, Nola and many other times. Now, he wasn't completely annihilated but Fabius Maximus considered these engagements as success, because they were.  
>   
> Iwo Jima example is dumb because that Bradley guy isn't commander of supreme allied forces or anything. No one knows or cares about that guy.   
>   
> Well, we have archeology that has minted coins of arround 217 BC in Etruscia which corresponds to Polybius account of Carthaginians minting coins while on the campaign. Those coins are also Phoenician coins and depict their elephants. Etruscians don't know what elephants are. We also have coins regarding Hamilcar, we have settlement proofs that he conquered Turdulii and southern Portugal. But the best evidence is actually elephant remains in Alps:  
>   
> The “Mahaney Layer” – High-altitude soil anomaly (2016 study)  
>   
> "A research team (Mahaney et al., 2016, Archaeometry) found:  
>   
> A 3,000 m elevation site near Col de la Traversette  
>   
> Disturbed soils containing:  
>   
> high levels of Clostridia bacteria associated with horse/donkey manure,  
>   
> trampled sediment consistent with a very large number of animals,  
>   
> radiocarbon dates around 216 BC, matching Hannibal’s invasion.  
>   
> This is not definitive proof, but it is the strongest scientific evidence ever found suggesting a large animal-driven force crossed the area around the time Hannibal did."

> @thomasellysonting3554 This argument misunderstands the basic timeline and politics of Carthage. Hannibal wasn’t ‘installed’ by Rome he was chosen by the Carthaginians after the war, and Rome initially forbade him from holding military office. They didn’t appoint him king. Carthage made him a civilian magistrate because he was popular.
  
> 
  
> And Rome absolutely did NOT trust him. The moment his economic reforms started helping Carthage recover, Rome threatened war again and forced him into exile. That’s the opposite of ‘Rome rewarded Hannibal.’
  
> 
  
> Also, the comparison to Osama Bin Laden is crazy. Carthage wasn’t a terrorist cell. It was a major state Rome needed stable enough to pay indemnities. Historically, powerful empires always prefer a beaten rival led by someone competent enough to manage the payments but politically weak enough to pose no military threat. Hannibal fit that role perfectly: a brilliant general with no army, no navy, and no political base left.
  
> 
  
> Polybius and Livy make perfect sense once you understand Carthaginian internal politics. The ending of the war doesn’t disprove their accounts, it confirms them: Rome won, broke Carthage, neutered Hannibal, and monitored him until he fled and then he died in exile with rome pressuring other states to turn him over. Nothing about that is inconsistent.

Hi Professor, I generally tend to agree with you, but this one is a bit much for me hehe, the fact that Cannae didn't leave any archaeological remains doesn't mean much, actually for most ancient famous battles  there is no archaeological evidence, there's many reasons for this but here's a few:

- Ancient battles were short-lived events

- They involved portable, reusable materials

- The ground was not permanently altered

- Time, farming, cities, and erosion erase traces

Also there's a big difference between having a river on your back and having a army of armed horsemen instead, with the river on the back the men can concentrate their effort forward, the river will not come an stab you at the rear, its a very different situation, if you have to push on 2 directions its a lot more difficult to pull off

I agree on the early stories of the early republic/kingdom to be just myths, but the punic wars are not the same.

Same thing about Caesar, it would have been almost impossible for him to pull off a fake story of his gallic conquest, because there were a lot of people coming and going from and back to Rome and not all of them were favorable to him, with the incredibly toxic political environment back at Rome it would have been impossible for him not to get exposed as a charlatan if his story wasn't rather accurate in general.

Hello prof. Jiang. The hoplites developed to fight on a mountainous terrain. The Peloponnese is not flat. Modern day Greece is 70% mountainous. I will not venture to explain why and how legionnaires defeated hoplites on the battlefield, as I am not an expert on the subject. > **@Blackmagician96** | 👍 6 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y-gXVzwbFMU&lc=UgwGtUwMQ4fFZtOU9Nt4AaABAg.APzS2uowR3pAPzwZ8Zv4Vg)  
>
> I think the professor meant to say phalanx, which is the 'Greek' system used by Macedon, made popular by Phillip and Alexander, the phalanx is not suitable for hilly terrain as your soldiers need to be close together and form a solid line. The maniple system is what made the legionnaires superior, in which the legionnaires form three lines in a checkerboard formation, this allows the the troops to be recycled in drawn out battles ensuring fresh bodies are always at the front as opposed to the phalanx which rely on a single line to pin down and smash the opponent, this also allows the maniple system to be more flexible and manoeuvrable.  

> **@thomasellysonting3554** | 👍 7 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y-gXVzwbFMU&lc=UgwGtUwMQ4fFZtOU9Nt4AaABAg.APzS2uowR3pAQ-8Kfc7gLH)  
>
> The story of the Hoplite is very politicized and the subject of many foundational myths in the West, but the the idea it was a "mountain" formation had always been silly even though Greece has a lot of mountains.  
>   
> In reality Greek warfare was always more multidimensional, with sieges and even naval combat far outnumbering field battles by phalanx.   
>   
> The Phalanx instead really came into the vogue in the wars against Persia, as Persia did field huge field armies and fought open battles with them. Indeed, Greek Phalanxes were primarily saw action not in Greece, but in Anatolia and Persia as mercenaries for the Persians or fighting against the Persians in the Greek Anatolian city-states (modern Turkey used to be Greek).  
>   
> It was Alexander who really solidified the Phalanx mythology, since he fought field battles against the Persian Emperor and took his throne, although again its worth noting by this point the Macedonian Army actually had cavalry, missile troops, and other infantry types.  
>   
> In any case the Romans did in fact really first win against the Greek military system by sheer numbers. Pyrrhus was in fact brought to his knees despite winning so many battles because the Romans kept throwing bodies at him. Later Roman generals would beat Greek generals without losing as many men, but it really wasn't down to the special qualities of the phalanx or legion and more based on the skill of the individual commanders (of which Rome had a lot of awful ones until Caesar).  
>   
> In short, stop thinking all the little Rome: Total War tactics videoss made the legion better than the Phalanx. Instead it was often down to which side had a better general and understood their own army better. And while the Romans tended to have worse generals, they tended to be able to replace their losses or send new armies - so any Greek king who beat the Romans tended to lose eventually like Pyrrhus.

> There is a reason history and archaeology departments quarrel a lot. Historians actually have extremely poor standards of evidence - boiling down to "if someone wrote it down then it is evidence" with only varying (often little) scrutiny. > **@thomasellysonting3554**

> ​@Blackmagician96 the main advantage of the maniple was tactical mobility, which it did have over the phalanx. It had fewer men and was less dependent on remaining a solid block.  
>   
> The checker board thing though was always a bizarre inclusion that never made any sense. All that formation did was to open each individual maniple to being flanked and beaten individually unless the second row immediately rushed forward to stop the flankers; in which case it would just turn into a line battle. Indeed by the time it is described by Roman historians it was definitely no longer being used, so I'd chalk it up as something they made up or misunderstood (eg maniples may have some small gaps between their lines to allow missile troops a lane to withdraw into).

Roman culture was more than military, gladiators and orgies. Though violent, arrogant and full of contradictions it had produced famous authors and thinkers such as Tacitus, Cicero, Horaz, Ovid, Musonius Rufus, Livy, Seneca, Pliny, Marcus Aurelius etc. Its administration, law making, engineering, architecture and infrastructure etc. were outstanding and shaped the modern (Western) world. For the first time in history the whole Mediterranean space was unified into one system with the universal idea of Roman citizenship irrespective of one’s origin. Tempora mutantur et nos in illis.

### **@Herzquelle** | 👍 7 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y-gXVzwbFMU&lc=Ugylhkz7vX5syo88hUt4AaABAg)

Sorry but :P Cannae didn’t happen is just wrong, we have too much hard evidence for that. Polybius Histories 3.107–118 (a Greek, not Roman propaganda) literally describes the battle from interviews with survivors, Livy AUC 22, Appian Hannibalic War and Plutarch Fabius line up with it. Archaeology backs everything: mass graves from the 3rd century BCE with sword and spear trauma (Quagliati 1928, Cazzella & Recchia 1990–98), Punic and Roman weapon fragments and Balearic sling bullets exactly where Polybius placed the armies (Barker et al. British School at Rome). Roman camp earthworks show up in geophysical scans. Barcid coinage from Iberia used to pay Hannibal’s troops was found in Apulia (Villaronga 1994, Jenkins 1975). Even the Roman census collapses right after 216 BCE (Brunt Italian Manpower, Beloch Römische Geschichte). The numbers may be exaggerated, but the battle absolutely happened, the evidence is overwhelming.

And yeah, Rome absolutely used Cannae later as propaganda. The battle happened, but the framing was political. Livy and later Roman writers inflated the casualty numbers to turn Hannibal into a mythic monster and justify total war. The phrase Hannibal ad portas became a psychological weapon to unify the population. Polybius himself notes Roman dramatization, and later elites used Cannae to push for harsher policies. By the Third Punic War, Cato and others used the memory of Cannae as proof that Carthage must be destroyed, even though Carthage was already weak. Roman speeches in Appian’s Punic Wars and fragments from Cato’s orations show how they kept invoking Hannibal and Cannae to manufacture fear and justify eliminating a former rival. The event was real, the numbers and narrative became a tool.

Which in return is in line with the core concepts of the theories and informatiuon Jiang shares with us.

4:07 

That’s fundamentally not true, both in terms of material culture and the history of agriculture in the peninsula. It was suitable for grain farming, particularly in the south, and has extremely fertile volcanic soils surrounding Rome (Etruria was build around and near extinct volcanic lakes), and throughout Campania and in the island of Sicily, and a more mild and negotiable seasonal weather pattern than Greece; a land truly poor, that is but a pile of rocks with small pockets of arable land, where most the land is negotiated by resinous, slow-growing flora. 

The ancient Greeks even wrote about the fertility and abundance of Italy. It was replete with rich agriculture/viticulture/animal herding in all of the societies that inhabited it before the founding of Rome. It was even written as being resource rich; sulphur was almost infinite, marble and granite were of supreme quality, and even jade was mined in the northern provinces of the peninsula.

You do not need much training as Roman soldier ? That is not true. The Romans put much emphasis on training. That was their strength, fighting with discipline and in closed formations but not as inflexible as the Phalanx. In the Roman Republic the legionares bought their own equipment. The pricipes  and triarii were well equipped. the hastati and velites were less well equipped and younger and poorer. After later military reforms the Romans changed that and the state provided for the equipment and standardized it. So the Roman army which Caesar took with him into Gallia was a very different one than the army which fought Hannibal.

> ​​@obinowankenobi I'm sorry that is way too long to read so I had to skim through it. In historical anlysis, we consider archeological evidence as first hand evidence. Writings by historians are called "second hand" (eg Xenophon and Ctesias who were present in the location they wrote about) or sometimes "third hand" (eg Herodotus who said himself he heard most of what he wrote based on what others told him; and that those others in turn, had heard the stories from others themselves). >
> ​@user-nz6ps4pz4n​sorry that I can't make this shorter, but the truth rarely makes for good one liners. Prof. Jiang's (and your) argument seems to boil down to:  
>   
> 1. In Chinese History there was a tradition of commanders putting their own armies in a position where they can't retreat forcing them to fight to the death and this was a successful tactic for these generals, so why did this same psychology not help the Romans?  
> 2. There's no archeological evidence that the battle of Cannae actually happened.  
> 3. Greek and Roman historians were known to lie.  
> Therefore, the Battle of Cannae never happened.  
>   
> To tackle the first point, the battle of Cannae was not equivalent to the situations in Chinese Military history that Prof. Jiang was talking about because in the Chinese examples, it is the generals themselves putting their army against a metaphorical wall preventing desertion and CHOOSING THE FORMATION OF THEIR ARMY TO BENEFIT THEM (caps for emphasis). In the Battle of Cannae, what we are told happened was that a Roman militia with minimal training was sent against Hannibal's professional army. Hannibal purposely used his best troops against the weakest parts of the Roman forces. The Roman center pushed back the Carthagenian center, while the Roman flanks were decimated, resulting in the Carthagenian forces enveloping the Romans and decimating them. Note that since there was no geographical barrier, plenty of Romans could have deserted from the rear before the envelopment was complete. In the Chinese examples, the generals themselves choose the rules of engagement, in Cannae, it was Hannibal who chose them not the Romans.  
>   
> On to the second point, if archeological evidence is the only point of evidence that you will accept, then you also have to reject the existence of the battle of Thermopylae, the Battle of Marathon, and the entirety of the Peloponnesian War (all of which Prof. Jiang accepts the existence of), because we don't have archeological evidence for those either. Battles don't usually leave mass graves or inscriptions that say "Here was the battle of such and such on the x day of y month of the nth year of so and so's reign." Historians don't just rely on one piece of evidence, they rely on multiple corroborating pieces of evidence and do what essentially amounts to forensic analysis. Cannae is considered very well attested by historians. We have multiple sources, all with different biases and agendas, all telling us that this battle happened, one of which being Polybius, who interviewed eyewitnesses on both sides. The Roman eyewitnesses were presumably those who got away before the envelopment, played dead, or were captured and the accounts of the Carthagenians leaving no survivors were then likely exaggerations. (Slight tangent: you do realize that eyewitness accounts are also considered primary sources, right?)  
>   
> Onto the final point: why would the Romans make up a military loss that made them look bad? If the Battle of Cannae never happened, why did the Romans change their strategy to completely avoid Hannibal? Why did several of Rome's allies switch sides? The fact that the Romans spoke openly about their defeats gives more credibility to them, not less. Any failures would have tarnished their reputation, not elevated it. There are plenty of details about Cannae that are debated, including the actual size of the armies and casualty numbers, but no serious historian argues that Cannae never happened. If it didn't, then none of the history of the Second Punic War after it would make sense.  
>   
> Prof. Jiang is trained as a political scientist, not a historian. Him insisting on the absolute necessity of archeological evidence betrays either a lack of understanding of the work of actual historians or him selectively ignoring evidence that contradicts his narrative. (Something that becomes more and more common as this lecture series progresses.) Anyone can post their opinions on the Internet, however wrong they may be. Correcting those errors usually takes ten times the time, or in other words "Lies can spread half way around the world before truth has the time to put on its pants".

Hi Prof,

I agree that the Romans were often a destructive force compared to their neighbors, but the assertion and logic behind the claim that the Battle of Cannae did not occur, and that Hannibal was a fairy tale is a bit perplexing.

Your logic seems to be that because Hannibal acted irrationally in the aftermath of his victories, this implies that he didn’t exist. By that reasoning, Pearl Harbor would also be a fabricated event because the attack is illogical from our perspective.

Further, you argue that the Romans fabricated the event to justify war with Carthage. However, when states seek to justify war, this is typically done by sending a small force into enemy territory (Mexican–American War) or by allying with a third party already in conflict with the enemy (First Punic War). For the Roman Senate to invent the annihilation of 100,000 Roman soldiers on Roman soil would have been easily disprovable at the time and far beyond what would be necessary to justify war.

There are also multiple third-party accounts (e.g., Polybius) that attest to the existence of Hannibal and the battles he fought. In addition, rich oral traditions in Roman society would have made it difficult to fabricate major events surrounding the Second Punic War.

The fact that mass graves have not been found does not prove the event was fabricated either. The common funerary practice at the time was cremation, which would reduce bodies to ash. Even men of high rank, including Roman emperors, were cremated.

I really enjoy your histories. In school there is often a hyper-fixation on dates, events, and timelines, and this style of learning isn’t very thought-provoking. I appreciate how your lectures have the ability to open the mind.

Thank you.

Jiang’s take on Rome as an ‘anti-civilization’ or ‘evil empire’ built on hate is intriguing, but it dives deep into pseudohistory: claiming the Battle of Cannae (a well-documented 216 BCE slaughter with archaeological evidence like mass graves near the Aufidus River) ‘never happened’ and was invented by Polybius to justify genocide? Or that Hannibal ‘didn’t exist’? That’s not scholarship—it’s conspiracy bait, cherry-picking classics like Livy and Virgil to fit a narrative that demonizes Western roots while ignoring Rome’s innovations in law, engineering, and governance.

This echoes his channel’s pattern: rewriting history as ‘nonsense’ propaganda (e.g., Aeneid as ‘piece of crap’ hate-fueled rewrite of Homer), blending game theory and ‘secret societies’ driven by guilt to explain empires, with nods to modern America as Roman-like barbarism. It’s entertaining, but misleads on facts—e.g., Caesar’s Gaul campaigns were brutal but not pure ‘genocide for slaves,’ and Roman citizenship expansion was pragmatic inclusion, not just militarism.

Reddit/X users call this out as radicalizing pseudohistory aligned with anti-Western tropes, often amplified by bot-like comment swarms (check the praise wall here—same phrases flooding in hours?).

For real Rome: Read Mary Beard’s SPQR (balanced on rise/fall without conspiracies) or Adrian Goldsworthy’s Cannae: Hannibal’s Greatest Victory (with battlefield archaeology debunking fabrications). 

Question ‘predictive’ narratives that scapegoat empires—history’s messier. #Pseudohistory #RomeFacts”

> **@Badbentham** | 👍 4 likes

>
> ​@thomasellysonting3554 Early Rome is fairly specific: A Republic, but with a nobility. Which means that the army consisted mostly of conscripted peasants, while the leaders were "professionalized", and had a vested interest in expansive warfare. During the first centuries, service in the army was indeed quite beneficial for the majority of the citizenship, as it provided ample chances for social upward-mobility. The contradictions of the social model, of part-time warriors, part-time farmers, however, became apparent after the Punic Wars, where the social elites fortified their economical and political status, while the traditional farmers struggled to survive against the now dominant large-scale slave industry, and with their military duties that could now take multiple years of their time ; - time they could not work for their families, allowing the elites to buy up their lands cheaply. -- The Roman army prior to Sulla and Marius could be considered as "semi-professional" ; it not only helped to turn Rome into an Empire, as it could field huge and 'somewhat' experienced armies. It also helped to destroy the very foundation of the Republic, the self-sufficient farmer, as the proclaimed ideal of the state since Romulus. It could probably be mentioned that social status as a whole, and the voting system in particular, was based on personal (financial) contributions to the war effort of the Republic; the tax system  developed over time, and only peaked, out of necessity, during the Empire.

Honestly, this was a pretty disappointing take on Roman history. It relies too much on broad generalizations and shaky assumptions to reach conclusions that feel oversimplified.



I understand that in a lecture you can’t cover everything and some level of simplification is necessary. But even then, framing Rome as nothing more than violence, lies, and abuse ignores just how complex and nuanced its history actually is.

### **@Mateohist** | 👍 3 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y-gXVzwbFMU&lc=UgwO6smG1zXETgdKDPx4AaABAg)

Hi!

Your video was very entertaining—I laughed a lot, especially at the bit with the hand and the sarcasm. I’d like to add a few points. The issue is that you present the Romans as barbarians from what is essentially a presentist perspective. I didn’t realize the Greeks didn’t enslave people or colonize cities, or practice forms of apartheid within them—terms you would probably use from a presentist standpoint when referring to the “Indiketes.”

Naturally, antiquity was brutal. The Assyrians left us entire walls depicting decapitations and the subjugation of peoples; the Romans also massacred populations. However, they did not destroy the ethnic substrate as you suggest. There is abundant epigraphic evidence showing the survival of indigenous peoples who gradually assimilated into Roman culture—a system of laws and a common language that extended from Eboracum (York) to Syria. It is easy to talk about how barbaric the Romans were from your perspective, just as it is easy to idealize Athens.

It was interesting to see how a non-European person views the Roman Empire, especially without mentioning aqueducts, baths, roads, theatres, temples, or sewers (there was more than just gladiators).

Honestly, it has been very useful for me as a source of humorous examples for my students—but also as a reminder of how the history of Rome can be framed through a lens of hostility toward foreigners, despite the fact that social mobility for slaves in Rome was significantly greater than in Greece.

There is a lot of nonsense in this lecture. No rome was not poor by the 3rd century BC, and after that got very rich very quickly. Central Italy is fabulous farmland with abundant people and reasorces. Romans were not poorly equipped. In fact they were the best armed, trained, and professional military of the day by far without equal. Their entire society was a war machine. Their legions were also construction crews. They built roads and bridges on campaigns, and slept in fortresses they built each and every night while marching on campaign. Hannibal Barca was a real person who did invade Italy. In the late republic Rome became on of the few societies ever to have a permanant military of professional soldiers trained, payed, and armed by the Government, that promised pensions, land, and citizenship for veterans. They began by copying Macedonian phalanxes, but could not emulate the companion cavalry effectively, so they innovated. They abandoned the phalanx and the syriza pike, and adopted a new formation called the maniple (we still dont known how they rotated ranks of legionaries in battle, but all sources say they did so) and developed a specialized javelin and sword combo that made them lethal at close and mid range. And they were extremely well armored and resilient so to close the distance on archers.

### **@szymonbaranowski8184** | 👍 4 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y-gXVzwbFMU&lc=UgyxqDhA5mXtJplfefR4AaABAg)

Major Historical Lies and Distortions:
1. The Battle of Cannae "Never Happened"

False Claim: The speaker argues the Battle of Cannae (216 BCE) was invented by Polybius to justify Roman imperialism.

Reality: Cannae is one of the most well-documented battles of antiquity, referenced by multiple contemporary and near-contemporary sources including Livy and Polybius. Archaeological evidence, while challenging to locate for ancient battles, exists in the form of mass graves and historical records.

2. Hannibal "Didn't Exist"

False Claim: The speaker suggests Hannibal Barca was a fictional creation.

Reality: Hannibal's existence is confirmed by multiple independent sources, including Roman, Greek, and Carthaginian records. His campaigns are among the best-documented of the ancient world.

3. All Roman History is "Complete Nonsense"

False Claim: The blanket statement that all Roman history is fabricated.

Reality: While Roman historians certainly had biases and engaged in propaganda, modern scholarship uses multiple methods (archaeology, comparative sources, textual criticism) to establish reliable historical facts.

4. Roman Military Tactics "Make No Sense"

False Claim: That double-envelopment tactics were impossible in ancient warfare.

Reality: The pincer movement was a known and effective military tactic used successfully in various ancient battles beyond Cannae.

Important Omissions and Misrepresentations:
1. Roman Cultural and Legal Contributions

The speaker completely ignores Rome's massive contributions to law, engineering, architecture, literature, and governance that shaped Western civilization.

2. Complexity of Roman Society

Reduces Rome to a simple "hate-based" society, ignoring its complex social structures, philosophical traditions, and cultural achievements.

3. Economic Complexity

Oversimplifies Roman economy as purely slave-based and war-driven, ignoring its sophisticated trade networks, currency systems, and agricultural practices.

4. Historical Context

Fails to acknowledge that many "Roman" practices (slavery, imperialism, brutal warfare) were common throughout the ancient world, not unique to Rome.

5. Archaeological Evidence

Dismisses substantial archaeological evidence that supports the historical record of Roman expansion and specific events like the Punic Wars.

6. Roman Republicanism

Ignores Rome's development of republican government and concepts of citizenship that influenced later democracies.

### **@Antonio_DG** | 👍 5 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y-gXVzwbFMU&lc=Ugya7ApPR4Rn-Oo4CVF4AaABAg)

Your "anti-civilization" thesis is a work of fantasy, not history. Here are the key errors:



- Battle of Cannae: It's one of the most documented battles in ancient history. Claiming it was invented is like denying D-Day happened. The archaeological and historical consensus is overwhelming.

- Romans "Invented" by Greeks: This is nonsense. While heavily influenced by Greece, Rome had unique, monumental achievements in law, engineering, and administration that shaped the Western world.

- "Hatred" of Greek Culture: The Roman elite were bilingual and admired Greek culture. The poet Horace famously wrote, "Graecia capta ferum victorem cepit" ("Captured Greece captured her savage conqueror"). They preserved and spread Greek learning, they didn't hate it.

- Fall of Rome: The "internal hate" theory is a vast oversimplification. Historians cite a complex mix of barbarian pressures, economic collapse, political instability, and plague.

- Jesus as a "Response": This is a theological simplification. Christianity emerged from 1st-century Judaism and spread due to complex social and religious factors within the Roman world.



In short, your narrative is a provocative story, but it ignores all historical evidence to fit its theme.

One explanation I heard about the battle of Cannae was that the faster light infantry units of the Carthaginians forced the Roman’s to break up their formation which made them sitting ducks. An explanation for Hannibal’s decision to invade the Italian mainland was that he identified the problem of Roman dominance as their infinite manpower which they largely drew from the other Italian cities. Hannibal thought if he came to the Italian mainland and defeated the Romans he could get the other Italians to rebel against the Roman’s and would therefore cut off Romes source of manpower.

>
> Because like most theories that contradict the official narrative it is always attacked and suppressed.  
>   
> History is generally NOT a profession that promotes critical thinking. A great example of this is the narrative surrounding the Battle of Midway in World War 2. For decades the official US Navy position was that it was simply sheer blind luck that let them win, and it was a miraculous victory.  
>   
> Almost all of that was overturned when Parshall published "Shattered Sword", which showed that the US narrative was already disproven in Japan decades ago; but it was completely ignored and even suppressed by the US Navy.   
>   
> Essentially, the Japanese were fully aware they could be ambushed and lose 4 carriers. They even had a war game before the battle where a junior officer (playing the Americans) sank two Japanese carriers using almost the same approach as the Americans in the real battle.  
>   
> However the Japanese admirals succumbed to hubris and ignored all these warnings, and thus walked into defeat.  
>   
> All of this evidence was actively ignored and suppressed by the US Navy, whose historical head had written the "miracle victory" narrative and basically disapproved any thesis that even suggested this may be wrong. Thats why it was an outsider and non-professional historian who had to bring up all the Japanese histories already written and published in Japan decades ago; which finally forced the US Navy to accept the revised narrative (the old historical head finally dying also opened the door to revisions).  
>   
> With Rome its much the same. The dominant historical school for Rome is all about focusing on its glory. They really don't want to talk about how demented the whole system is and it is absolutely ridiculous to claim there was a "Roman Peace" when they literally had a century-long non-stop civil war called the Third Century Crisis.

### **@pbutter81** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y-gXVzwbFMU&lc=Ugyi1CEUsKXwVzhAYGJ4AaABAg)

I'd like to preface this by saying I'm a big fan of Professor Jiang's YouTube lectures. He says early on that you should treat a lot of these ideas as theories. But the Battle of Cannae idea proposed in this video struck me as really off. Here's why:

-Dead bodies were usually looted after battles. Spears, shields, swords, rings, anything that could be recycled or resold was fair game. 
-The Aufidus / Ofanto rivers changed course, so if the bodies were stripped of everything material, the bones could dissolve in the water. Plus there was a lot of agricultural activity throughout post-Canna periods, bones can dissolve in wet soil too. 
-Spear tips that are definitely Iberian-made were recovered from the site. I guess theoretically some other military figure with Iberian weapons could've gone to that area and left them there, but it seems awfully coincidental that a member of the Barca family with a tremendous Iberian presence is supposedly never there but Iberian spear tips are. Like...wow. Amazing coincidence! 
-Other sources verify that Rome raised slave legions and lowered property requirements for service after the battle's date. Why would they do that if not for this battle? A really radical change in policy for no reason?  
-Multiple historians wrote about this event. Polybius, Livy, Plutarch, etc. That's a lot of coordinated lying. 
-Isn't propaganda that justifies cruelty to an enemy capable of being much less revelatory about your incompetence? The summary is, "We got our asses kicked by a superior general who slaughtered something like 50,000 of us." You don't need to make your propaganda so humiliating. You could just be like, "Those Carthaginians are evil for X,Y,Z reasons, that's why we killed them." Rome looks really inferior and incompetent with "propaganda" like this. 

Anyway. Still a fan, but the Battle of Cannae definitely happened.

### **@jameslove-vani797** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y-gXVzwbFMU&lc=Ugy37L4BRYSHAtavJmN4AaABAg)

Big fan of this show; the following isn't a critique.

The bit about why Hannibal never became king is speculative, and there is plenty of evidence to show why he did not try.
Hannibal won the biggest battle in history up to that point (assuming it took place, according to this class), but lacked the man power and resources to take Rome, which was enormous even at this point of its history.
So he sent word back to Carthage for reinforcements, which were denied for political reasons; basically, Hannibal wasn't following proper form or etiquette in his actions against the Romans, and had developed enemies in their version of a Senate (I can't recall off hand what it was called).
Instead of supplying Hannibal with reinforcements and resources, Carthage instead expressed dismay. Not at his success, but at his lack of asking permission on how to proceed.
He wasn't following political heirarchies.
So they turned their back on him, denied future help, and the Romans were eventually able to overcome Hannibal, who simply ran out of troops and usable equipment.

Hannibal did not become a Carthaginian ruler because the ruling class was threatened by his success, recognized what he'd become (their replacement) were he able to succeed against Rome and return the hero, and so prevented the general from the mission they set him out to do in the first place.
They wrongly assumed Rome could never defeat them, and decided to accept a temporary loss against Rome by sacrificing their greatest general, Hannibal, on behalf of their (fucking) careers.

Not long after, Rome invaded Carthage, sacked it, killed its citizens, burned it to the ground and then *salted the remains*.

It was written as a political effort to create a powerful foundational myth and a hero model for soldiers (totally not a copy of the Greek one), but it really didn't stick, maybe because it was too late for that, but also because is kind of depressing as it doesn't have a happy end.

I agree with the argument that the main struggle is between the upper and lower nobility, but the reason why the poor don't rebel isn't because they are stupid or helpless, it is for two reasons; firstly they are being manipulated (divide and conquer), but more importantly, they don't have time since they struggle to survive. If you're just chasing paychecks, you have no time to bother with revolution. Unless that is your last chance, of course. But more to the point, this plight of the poor was beautifully described by Oscar Wilde, The Soul of a Man (under Socialism), which I recommend this class to read. "There is only one class in the community that thinks more about money than the rich, and that is the poor. The poor can think of nothing else. That is the misery of being poor."

> **@Badbentham**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y-gXVzwbFMU&lc=UgyocRV3Kb-DK7lT2wZ4AaABAg.APzGnFF4e_uAQ-Ua8NZDvR)  
>
> I find the Normans are a fairly good example: The Vikings took Normandy, settled, became the aristocracy, and had children. Roughly a hundred years later, larger parts of the families, well-trained soldiers , got kicked out; no place in the family succession. They went to Southern Italy, where they first served as mercenaries for the different factions, Lombards, Church, Byzantium. A few decades later, they had large enough numbers, and conquered the territory for themselves. -- In this case, the Norman elites violently replaced the existing ones; the noble families of Normandy successfully externalized their "problems". The alternative for the noblemen  would have been to go to war with their brethren at home. -- The general gist of the story is that the social elites of state-like entities generally prefer to externalize their internal conflicts over status and power, before the different power factions inside a given territory fight a Civil War, or someone starts a political revolution. -- Alas: Probably the most graphic example was the global conquest of Dschingis Khan's family. They united all their forces, so that all major heirs could rule over a huge empire, so that at least the following generations would have no succession crisis: The Mongols lived polygamous, and had huge families. -- After 1242, and a final meeting at home, the family members went their own way.

Hello prof,

I wanted to comment on something you said earlier in one of the lectures about Islam. You mentioned that Islam teaches that God is everywhere or in a person who follows the pillars of Islam. Since you’ve shown sympathy toward monotheism and Islam, I thought I’d share a small clarification from the Islamic perspective.

In Islamic belief, God is completely separate from His creation. He isn’t inside anything, and He isn’t spread out everywhere in space or the universe. He exists outside of time, space, and all created dimensions. So the idea of God being “in” someone, or being “everywhere,” goes against the original Islamic creed. Islamic creed (aqeedah) is built on 3 core principles: 

(a) Lordship: God is the Creator; everything else is created;
(b) Worship: worship is directed to Him alone, not to anything within creation; 
(c) Names and Attributes: we affirm the names and qualities God described Himself with, without giving Him traits of the creation or giving creation divine traits. Some divine names share a wording with attributes of the creation, but their reality isn’t comparable. For example, God is “The Merciful,” but His mercy isn’t like human mercy. Or God mentions having a “face,” but that face is not like a human face. In this life, we cannot imagine or comprehend Him, because nothing resembles Him. In the next life, the greatest reward for believers will be seeing God.

Historically, there were philosophers and mystics who tried to import pantheistic or monistic ideas into Islam, and traces of those ideas still show up today. But they were never compatible with the actual Islamic creed, and were always considered outside the bounds of original belief system.

Because of this, Islam is not monistic. It draws a sharp line between Creator and creation. Even though God knows everything and controls everything, He does not inhabit things. I’m sharing this just as a friendly clarification, since your explanation leaned more toward a monistic view.

>
> @nerf2752 says, "Gladiators' arena, Origies and how Rome ended by printing money"  
>   
> Most gladiator fights were not lethal, with estimates suggesting death occurred in only about 10% to 20% of matches. Gladiators were expensive to train and replace, so their owners often preferred them to survive and fight again, making them valuable assets. Instead of constant death, the fights were often a display of skill and endurance, and a downed but not killed gladiator might be spared if they fought bravely.   
>   
> Religious Rites vs. Orgies: The term orgia originally referred to ecstatic religious ceremonies in ancient Greek religion, particularly those honoring Dionysus (Bacchus). These were not primarily sex parties but spiritual events involving wine, music, and dancing intended to achieve a state of religious ecstasy or possession by the god.  
>   
> Scandal and Suppression: The secretive nature of these rites and the participation of women led to suspicion and moral panic, particularly after Roman society was influenced by these Greek cults. The Roman Senate famously suppressed the Bacchic rites in 186 B.C. based on rumors of sexual and other debaucheries, though scholars still debate the extent of the actual sexual content.  
>   
> Propaganda and Slander: Historical accounts, such as those by the historian Livy, may have exaggerated the events to justify the suppression and portray the cults as a threat to Roman social order. Later, political opponents or moral reformers also used accusations of sexual depravity and decadence to slander rivals.  
>   
> Misinterpretation: The modern idea of "orgy" as a wild sex party is a misunderstanding of the term's original religious meaning. While sexual activity may have occurred at some of these ecstatic rituals, it wasn't the sole or even primary purpose, and the myths we have today were amplified by later interpretations and political maneuvering.

gemini: While time and erosion have erased most traces of temporary camps, modern methods have provided significant proof:

Geochemical Analysis (Animal Manure): In 2016, a team of scientists found layers of compacted manure in an Alpine pass (Col de la Traversette) dating exactly to 218 BCE. DNA analysis showed the presence of thousands of horses and pack animals in a location where such a large group had no reason to be, unless it was an army.

Coins and Weaponry: Carthaginian coins and arrowheads have been discovered in Spain and southern France, matching the recorded route of the campaign.

Battlefields: At the Battle of Cannae, where Hannibal famously crushed the Roman legions, archaeological surveys have identified mass graves and remnants of military equipment that confirm the sheer scale of the slaughter.

>
> ​​@AAmdaz-j3k the issue here is that the evidence of Hannibal's link to Carthage is spotty. Note that based on Roman accounts all of Hannibal's reinforcements had to walk to Italy from Spain, not Carthage.  
>   
> This overland reinforcement route was supposedly due to Rome controlling the seas, but this is contradicted also by the official Roman version of events because Hannibal was able to simply sail from Italy to Carthage at the end of the Punic War to fight Scipio at Zama.  
>   
> Personally, I think Hannibal and his family were of Carthaginian origin, but were no longer directly linked to Carthage and were instead running their own empire in Spain. Carthage had accounts of mercenaries revolting and doing their own thing, and so its not out of the question the Hanno family was part of these mutinies.  
>   
> Thats also why if you look at the debates in the official Roman history, there is actually a debate as to whether the main goal should be to defeat Hannibal (Fabian's faction) or to capture Carthage (Scipio's faction).  
>   
> It may very well be that Scipio simply attacked Carthage for his own profit even though they were not actually involved in this war. Note that Scipio was actually convicted of corruption after the Punic War - he took bribes from an enemy king - which most modern historians dismiss as a show trial but in reality may have been the truth.   
>   
> Thing is these kinds of inquiries are never seriously explored by modern "Roman" historians because their focus for the past two centuries has been to insist that Roman history is real and important rather than seriously analyzing even just the texts we have. Thats why you will almost never hear any modern Roman historian seriously entertain the possibility that Scipio was in fact corrupt and lying; and that the "show trial" seemed unlikely when you consider it was pushed by Cato the Elder who was known to be quite the straight arrow and thus is a valid interpretation given the current texts we have available.

This school teacher tends to misinterpret—or simply dismiss—well-established historical facts quite casually. For example, he claims that the Romans invented the defeat of their own army of 50,000 men at Cannae merely as a justification to invade and destroy Carthage, which he says was richer than Rome—just because the exact geographic spot of the battle hasn’t been pinpointed.

We do know roughly where it took place, even if the precise location hasn’t been identified. But the teacher just says it was a Roman invention—end of story. He offers no further argument, simply erasing from history a battle that took place over 2,200 years ago.

And he does this with all of Roman history. According to him, everything is an invention of the Romans. That must be the kind of history they teach in China about the Roman Empire—to diminish it and present Chinese civilization as superior. It wouldn’t surprise me if that were actually the case.

### **@jtzoltan**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y-gXVzwbFMU&lc=Ugw3y3-JoEvyppZXAN94AaABAg)

Professor, you make it sound like Pyrrus of Epirus was just absolutely destroying the Romans in battle after battle. They only had 3 large battles and they were so hard fought and closely matched that Pryrrus only barely edged out a technical victory as the Romans withdrew in orderly fashion. It wasn't that Rome sent wave after wave to the slaughter.  They were disciplined, determined and capable foes that matched Pyrrus. Pyrrus is quoted as saying something like "Another such victory over the Romans, and we are undone".

Also,  Greeks had mountainous homelands and much of what they conquered except for Egypt and Mesopotamia was hilly. The Romans were relatively well armoured, even on the earlier days of the Republic. They also largely had their own property so they could buy their own equipment. Poorer members of the army often became the lightly armoured skirmishers called velites, armed with javelins and slings. They didn't start getting their equipment paid for until the Marian reforms in the first century BC. That  expanded manpower for the army to the landless proletariats and those seeking citizenship.

### **@axiotera** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y-gXVzwbFMU&lc=Ugx5bCS4NfNKDAOkvCd4AaABAg)

If you are going to call out other historians for assumptions, you cannot then introduce your own without sources.

How do you know there was equality in the early republic? The Conflict of the Orders happens almost immediately. Plebeian secessions, debt bondage, patrician monopolies, none of that looks like “no inequality, therefore no corruption.”

Double envelopment is “impossible”? Two lines of cavalry enveloping an overextended infantry block is not sorcery. You are just being incredulous because the story does not fit your narrative.

You are cherry picking Roman stories as accurate when they support your thesis, but dismissing Roman history as fabricated propaganda when it does not. Which is it?

You are using the Iliad to illustrate Greek virtues like love and heroism as central to Greek society, fine, but what about the atrocities in the Iliad?

You treat gladiators as proof of a uniquely Roman obsession with death, ignoring that Greek athletics included lethal events and that ritualized violence appears in most cultures.

You say Rome could not produce culture, then cite the Aeneid, written by a Roman, and ignore Roman law, engineering, rhetoric, architecture, philosophy, and the fact that Greek literature survives largely because Romans copied and taught it.

You imply Hannibal should have crowned himself king because “great generals always do.” That is vibes, not history. Not every successful commander turns into a king.

You say Hannibal might not have existed, yet Carthaginian coins and inscriptions literally reference Hannibal’s family. You cannot erase material evidence because the narrative is inconvenient.

You frame Polybius as a novelist inventing wars, but Polybius openly criticizes exaggeration and tries to verify events with witnesses. Reducing him to a cartoon propagandist is not an argument.

You claim Rome won because they were “poor and united,” then also claim Rome was corrupt and collapsing internally from the beginning. Those are not the same storyline. You cannot use both depending on which point you are trying to score.

You use modern psychology and contemporary ideology to diagnose ancient societies, then ignore ancient context entirely. Presentism doesn’t become valid just because it is dramatic.

You treat complexity as contradiction, then declare contradiction as proof of corruption. Civilizations can be brutal and innovative. Rome is not required to fit a single emotional thesis.

Technically there are indeed people, that experience no pain. So to those people placing their hand in burning fire is just another task to accomplish. So it's not safe to say, that it's impossible for this event to be plausible deniable.

I have met such a person myself during my stay in a psychiatric facility. 

Plus: Pain tolerance increases exponentially with consistent exposure. 

Reason: Desensitation of pain receptors. Just as with the majority of other receptors, pain receptors sensitivity exponentially decreases with exposure to it's destined neurotransmitter. Cortisol being an exception to the bunch.

So for a person, that spent their entire life fighting wars, training their combat abilities with real weaponry and against real soldiers it is not surprising, that their respective pain tolerance is vastly superior to the modern citizen, that has not experienced any struggles matching the one's a soldier from this age of time has gone through on a daily basis.

You also mentioned their brutality and the fact, that they always fought to the death. This means, that their pain tolerance was likely exceeding the benchmark of other countries during that time.

Meanwhile a king, that hasn't seen any of that can't even fathom the amount of pain resistence such a person has.

Talking from experience. I used to remove my own illnesses by taking a scissor and just cutting off the infected parts of my flesh. 

Tooth flesh? Gone. Parts of my feet? Removed. Pimples and other facial parts? Cut off. 
Pulling my own teeth? Indeed.

Mentality is also a big part of that. The difference between:

- This could happen
vs
- This HAS to happen

Putting your life on the line is the most effective way of winning.

Every battle is a bet. Like poker. Imagine it like an entry fee.

if [You want to fight] i'll [fight to the death and do not care about both my or your death] 

This creates an entry fee. The other party either has to match that, or they can't participate.

if [they'll kill me not caring about themselves] then [i must risk my own life fighting]

This creates a debt that is so high, that most people will not dare to initiate combat. And as such, you will always be on a higher bet, than the people wanting to battle you.

View war as a consumer. A black hole. Everything that doesn't kill me makes me stronger. If i win, i'll absorb both their strenght and their tactics plus their knowledge and add it to the mass i've been accumulating making me even stronger, each time i fight.

That is my mentality. Always choose exposure. This will grant you all the enemies knowledge they try to apply to you in order to break you. To view it as an opportunity to grow, rather than to withdraw.

The more capable you become, the more value you present to others, giving them less insent to get rid of you. So it's a win win.

Consume. Absorb. And replicate.

Each time you absorb, you decrease the risk of taking damage to the same thing. Means each time you absorb your risk of volunerability decreases. Until you wear an armor of knowledge so vast, that nothing can penetrate it's collective mass.
